\Cref{obj:thm:published-binary-collision-comparison} has two roles. First, it states the exact binary occupancy guarantee used for the comparison, including its bias and variance scales. Second, it shows that the selector's nonparametric term is bounded pointwise by the collision remainder, and that it is asymptotically smaller along sequences where the polynomial scale is negligible relative to the collision scale and the alphabet lies below \(n\log(en)\) in the displayed sense. This makes the comparison presentation-level: the headline bracket in \cref{obj:thm:two-sided-minimax-bracket-all-d} rests on the same-class upper and lower statements already assembled in \cref{obj:thm:frontier-upper-all-d,obj:thm:radius-channel-converse-all-d}.

\subsection{Future work}

The matched-regime summary in \cref{obj:thm:fixed-interior-tightness-and-shrinking-radius-gap-all-d} leaves a sharply described residual shrinking-radius wedge for further development. The following remark records the target problem in that region using the same notation as the regime theorem.

\begin{remarkv}{oeq:shrinking-radius-frontier}[Shrinking-radius frontier]
Let
\[
L:=\log(en),\qquad
b:=n^{-1}+\frac{d}{n^2},\qquad
u:=\frac{d^2}{n^2L^2}.
\]
In the residual shrinking-radius wedge
\[
b\ll u\ll1,
\qquad
b\ll\sigma^2\ll1,
\]
equivalently up to boundary constants
\[
\sqrt n\,L\ll d\ll nL,
\qquad
\sigma^2\gg b,
\]
a natural next question is whether one can either construct a realization-wise radius-constrained paired-cell moment-matching fuzzy experiment in \(\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) with ATE separation of order
\[
M\min\left\{\sigma,\frac{d}{nL}\right\}
\]
and sample-mixture total variation bounded away from one, or construct an explicit total estimator whose risk is strictly smaller in order than the selector benchmark
\[
M^2\{b+\min(u,\sigma^2)\},
\]
with the product benchmark
\[
M^2(b+\sigma^2u)
\]
as the target rate. The hypothesis-independent channel yields the product separation
\[
M\sigma\min\left\{1,\frac{d}{nL}\right\}.
\]
\end{remarkv}

\Cref{obj:oeq:shrinking-radius-frontier} records two possible mathematical routes in the residual shrinking-radius wedge. One route asks for a radius-constrained least-favorable construction with ATE separation at the selector scale; the other asks for an estimator whose risk improves on the current selector benchmark toward the product scale suggested by the channel lower bound. The divergence example in \cref{obj:thm:fixed-interior-tightness-and-shrinking-radius-gap-all-d} places \(d_n=n\) and \(\sigma_n=\log(en)^{-1/2}\) inside this region and gives logarithmic separation between the displayed selector and converse benchmarks.

\subsection{Limitations}

The results are scoped to fixed overlap, known outcome scale \(M\), known heterogeneity radius \(\sigma\), and scalar average treatment effect estimation. Within that scope, \cref{obj:thm:two-sided-minimax-bracket-all-d} gives risk bounds for clipped estimators in \(\mathcal T_{n,M}\) from \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk}, and \cref{obj:thm:frontier-upper-all-d} constructs a selector that uses the radius as an input. The inference target is mean-squared error for point estimation over \(\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\), so confidence intervals, adaptive radius selection, and data-driven choice of \(M\) are directions beyond the estimation bracket established here.

The moment and overlap assumptions also define the statistical environment in which the bracket should be read. Fixed overlap in \cref{obj:ass:overlap} keeps treatment and control assignment probabilities uniformly away from zero and one in every supported cell. Mean normalization and the second central moment envelope in \cref{obj:ass:mean-normalization,obj:ass:second-central-moment} set the real-outcome scale under weak conditional moment control. Approximate homogeneity in \cref{obj:ass:approximate-homogeneity} supplies the radius that determines which estimator branch is selected. Under exactly these conditions, the paper characterizes the delivered finite-sample minimax bracket and its matched regimes for arbitrary cell masses.


\appendix

\section*{Appendices}

\section{Binary source lemmas}

The lower-bound transfers in \cref{obj:prop:zeng-class-inclusion-and-lower-transfer,obj:lem:scaled-binary-exact-lower-transfer-all-d,obj:thm:radius-channel-converse-all-d} use binary discrete-adjustment experiments, originating in \citet{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}, as source problems. This appendix records the binary source lemmas in the notation used by the main text, with attribution and statistical context supplied by the citation. The first definition fixes the fixed-sample exactly homogeneous binary experiment and its binary average response contrast.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_3}[Fixed-sample binary homogeneous experiment]
For integers \(n,d\ge1\) and \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), let \(\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)\) be the fixed-sample binary-outcome exactly homogeneous source experiment consisting of the product laws \(P^{\otimes n}\) generated by laws \(P\in\mathcal H_d^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)\). Its binary average response contrast is
\[
\psi(P)=\frac1d\sum_{k=1}^d\{\mu_{1k}^{\mathrm{bin}}(P)-\mu_{0k}^{\mathrm{bin}}(P)\}.
\]
\end{definitionv}

\Cref{obj:synth_3} packages the source experiment used by the exact-homogeneity collision transfer. It aligns the fixed-sample product laws with the binary source classes introduced in \cref{obj:synth_2} and gives the target \(\psi(P)\) that is later scaled into the real-outcome average treatment effect through the affine embeddings.

The first finite-sample collision source lemma concerns the exactly homogeneous binary source experiment. It supplies the \(d/n^2\) component used in the exact-radius part of the real-outcome lower benchmark.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:binary-exact-collision-lower}[Binary collision lower bound]
Let \(u_{\epsilon}:=\min\{1/2,\,8(1/2-\epsilon)^2\}\).  Suppose that:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Sample and alphabet.)} \(n,d\in\mathbb N\) satisfy \(n\ge 1\) and \(d\ge 1\).
\item \textbf{(Overlap.)} The overlap parameter \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\) satisfies \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).
\item \textbf{(Collision regime.)} The dimensions satisfy \(d\le n^2\).
\end{itemize}
For the fixed-sample binary exactly homogeneous source experiment \(\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)\), generated by binary laws with uniform cell masses, propensities in \([\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\), and a common cell effect across all cells, the minimax mean-squared risk for estimating the binary average response contrast \(\psi(P)\) satisfies
\[
\frac{u_{\epsilon}^2}{64}\frac{d}{n^2}
\le
\inf_{\widehat\psi}
\sup_{P\in\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)}
E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[\{\widehat\psi-\psi(P)\}^2\right],
\]
where the infimum ranges over all measurable real-valued estimators \(\widehat\psi\) of the \(n\)-sample.
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:binary-exact-collision-lower}]
Put
\[
u_\epsilon:=\min\{1/2,8(1/2-\epsilon)^2\},
\qquad \Delta:=u_\epsilon\frac{\sqrt d}{n},
\qquad \rho:=\sqrt{\Delta/8}.
\]
The assumptions \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), \(1\le n\), \(1\le d\), and \(d\le n^2\) imply \(\sqrt d\le n\), and hence
\[
0<u_\epsilon,
\qquad 0\le \Delta\le u_\epsilon\le\frac12,
\qquad \rho^2=\frac{\Delta}{8},
\qquad \rho\le\frac14,
\qquad \rho\le\frac12-\epsilon,
\qquad \rho+\frac{\Delta}{4}\le\frac12 .
\]
% lean: binaryExactMinimaxRisk_collision_lower

For a sign pattern \(\xi\in\{\pm1\}^d\), define a binary law \(P_\xi\) as follows.  Each cell has mass \(p_k=1/d\), and
\[
\pi_k^{(\xi)}:=\frac12+\rho\xi_k,
\qquad
\mu_{1k}^{(\xi)}:=\frac12-\rho\xi_k+\frac{\Delta}{4},
\qquad
\mu_{0k}^{(\xi)}:=\frac12-\rho\xi_k-\frac{\Delta}{4}.
\]
For \(a,y\in\{0,1\}\), the one-observation probabilities are
\[
P_\xi(X=k,A=a,Y=y)
=\frac1d
\begin{cases}
\pi_k^{(\xi)},& a=1,\\
1-\pi_k^{(\xi)},& a=0,
\end{cases}
\begin{cases}
\mu_{ak}^{(\xi)},& y=1,\\
1-\mu_{ak}^{(\xi)},& y=0.
\end{cases}
\]
Let \(P_0\) be the same construction with \(\Delta=\rho=0\), so \(\pi_k=1/2\) and \(\mu_{1k}=\mu_{0k}=1/2\).  The displayed bounds put all propensities in \([\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\) and all conditional outcome probabilities in \([0,1]\).  Also
\[
\mu_{1k}^{(\xi)}-\mu_{0k}^{(\xi)}=\Delta/2
\]
for every cell \(k\).  Thus \(P_0\) and every \(P_\xi\) belong to \(\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)\), and
\[
\psi(P_\xi)=\Delta/2,
\qquad
\psi(P_0)=0 .
\]
% lean: exactSignExactLaw_ate

Define
\[
\Gamma:=\Delta-\frac{\Delta^2}{2}+\frac{\Delta^3}{8}.
\]
Since \(0\le \Delta\le1/2\),
\[
0\le\Gamma\le \Delta\le 1 .
\]
Moreover,
\[
\Delta^2
=u_\epsilon^2\frac d{n^2},
\]
and, since \(u_\epsilon\le1/2\),
\[
2n^2\left(\frac{\Gamma}{2}\right)^2
\le
\frac{n^2\Delta^2}{2}
=
\frac{u_\epsilon^2d}{2}
\le
\frac d8
\le
d\log2 .
\]
% lean: binaryExactMinimaxRisk_collision_lower

Let \(Q_0=P_0^{\otimes n}\), let \(Q_\xi=P_\xi^{\otimes n}\), and let \(Q_1\) be the uniform mixture of the \(Q_\xi\)'s.  For one observation, direct expansion of the preceding probabilities and \(\rho^2=\Delta/8\) give, for every pair \(\xi,\xi'\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\sum_{a,y\in\{0,1\}}
\frac{P_\xi(X=k,A=a,Y=y)P_{\xi'}(X=k,A=a,Y=y)}
{P_0(X=k,A=a,Y=y)} \\
&\quad=
\frac4d\,\pi_k^{(\xi)}\pi_k^{(\xi')}
\left[\mu_{1k}^{(\xi)}\mu_{1k}^{(\xi')}
+(1-\mu_{1k}^{(\xi)})(1-\mu_{1k}^{(\xi')})\right] \\
&\qquad+
\frac4d\,(1-\pi_k^{(\xi)})(1-\pi_k^{(\xi')})
\left[\mu_{0k}^{(\xi)}\mu_{0k}^{(\xi')}
+(1-\mu_{0k}^{(\xi)})(1-\mu_{0k}^{(\xi')})\right] \\
&\quad=
\frac1d+\frac{\Gamma}{d}\xi_k\xi'_k .
\end{aligned}
\]
Summing over cells gives the single-observation overlap
\[
\sum_{k,a,y}
\frac{P_\xi(X=k,A=a,Y=y)P_{\xi'}(X=k,A=a,Y=y)}
{P_0(X=k,A=a,Y=y)}
=
1+\frac{\Gamma}{d}\sum_{k=1}^d\xi_k\xi'_k .
\]
Therefore the finite-mixture chi-square identity is
\[
1+\chi^2(Q_1,Q_0)
=
2^{-2d}
\sum_{\xi,\xi'}
\left(
1+\frac{\Gamma}{d}
\sum_{k=1}^d\xi_k\xi'_k
\right)^n .
\]
We use the finite-sign Ingster bound in the following form: if \(\gamma\ge0\), \(2\gamma\le1\), and \(2n^2\gamma^2\le d\log2\), then
\[
2^{-2d}\sum_{\xi,\xi'}
\left(1+\frac{2\gamma}{d}\sum_{k=1}^d\xi_k\xi'_k\right)^n
\le 2 .
\]
With \(\gamma=\Gamma/2\), the hypotheses are exactly the preceding displays, so
\[
1+\chi^2(Q_1,Q_0)\le2,
\qquad\text{hence}\qquad
\chi^2(Q_1,Q_0)\le1 .
\]
Consequently,
\[
\operatorname{TV}(Q_0,Q_1)
\le
\frac12\sqrt{\chi^2(Q_1,Q_0)}
\le
\frac12.
\]
% lean: exact_tvDist_le_half

For any measurable estimator \(\widehat\psi\), the two-point testing inequality with parameter values \(0\) and \(\Delta/2\), testing radius \(\Delta/4\), and the displayed total-variation bound gives
\[
\frac14
\le
\max\left\{
Q_0\!\left(|\widehat\psi-0|\ge \Delta/4\right),
Q_1\!\left(|\widehat\psi-\Delta/2|\ge \Delta/4\right)
\right\}.
\]
Since \(Q_1\) is the uniform mixture over the \(Q_\xi\)'s, some sign pattern \(\xi\) satisfies
\[
Q_\xi\!\left(|\widehat\psi-\Delta/2|\ge \Delta/4\right)
\ge
Q_1\!\left(|\widehat\psi-\Delta/2|\ge \Delta/4\right).
\]
On the corresponding error event under \(Q_0\) or under this \(Q_\xi\), squared error is at least \((\Delta/4)^2\).  Hence
\[
\max\left\{
E_{Q_0}\widehat\psi^2,
E_{Q_\xi}(\widehat\psi-\Delta/2)^2
\right\}
\ge
\frac{\Delta^2}{64}.
\]
Taking the supremum over \(\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)\) and then the infimum over measurable estimators gives
\[
\frac{\Delta^2}{64}
\le
\inf_{\widehat\psi}
\sup_{P\in\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)}
E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[(\widehat\psi-\psi(P))^2\right].
\]
% lean: binaryExactMinimaxRisk_collision_aux

Using \(\Delta^2=u_\epsilon^2d/n^2\), this is exactly
\[
\frac{u_\epsilon^2}{64}\frac d{n^2}
\le
\inf_{\widehat\psi}
\sup_{P\in\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)}
E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[(\widehat\psi-\psi(P))^2\right].
\]
% lean: binaryExactMinimaxRisk_collision_lower
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:binary-exact-collision-lower} records the collision-scale binary statement associated with \citet{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}. The overlap-dependent constant \(u_{\epsilon}\) is fixed once \(\epsilon\) is fixed, and the displayed inequality gives a lower bound for estimating \(\psi(P)\) over uniform-mass exactly homogeneous binary laws when \(d\le n^2\).

A second exact-homogeneity source lemma records the same binary phenomenon with the parametric term included. This statement is used when the transfer argument carries the homogeneous source risk into the real-outcome class.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:zeng-binary-exact-homogeneity-lower}[Exact binary lower bound]
Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).  For integers \(n,d\) with \(d>0\), let \(\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)\) be the fixed-sample binary-outcome experiment with cell masses \(p_k=1/d\), overlap \(\epsilon\le \pi_k\le 1-\epsilon\), conditional response means in \([0,1]\), and a common cell treatment effect, and write
\[
\psi(P)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=1}^d(\mu_{1k}-\mu_{0k}).
\]
There exist constants \(a_\epsilon,b_\epsilon>0\) and an integer \(N_\epsilon\) such that, for every \(n,d\in\mathbb N\) with \(d>0\), \(N_\epsilon\le n\), and \(d\le b_\epsilon n^2\),
\[
a_\epsilon\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{d}{n^2}\right)
\le
\inf_{\widehat\psi}\sup_{P^{\otimes n}\in\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)}
E_P\!\left[(\widehat\psi-\psi(P))^2\right].
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:zeng-binary-exact-homogeneity-lower}]
Let the overlap-dependent collision constant be
\[
u_\epsilon:=\min\left\{\frac12,\,8\left(\frac12-\epsilon\right)^2\right\},
\qquad
a_\epsilon:=\min\left\{\frac1{200},\,\frac{u_\epsilon^2}{128}\right\},
\qquad
b_\epsilon:=1,
\qquad
N_\epsilon:=1 .
\]
Because \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), we have \(u_\epsilon>0\), and therefore \(a_\epsilon>0\); also \(b_\epsilon>0\).
% lean: zengBinaryExactHomogeneityLower

Fix \(n,d\in\mathbb N\) with \(d>0\), \(N_\epsilon\le n\), and \(d\le b_\epsilon n^2\).  Then \(n>0\) and, since \(b_\epsilon=1\),
\[
d\le n^2 .
\]
Write
\[
R_{n,d,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{bin}}
:=
\inf_{\widehat\psi}
\sup_{P\in\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)}
\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[(\widehat\psi-\psi(P))^2\right]
\]
for the fixed-sample binary exact-homogeneity minimax risk.

First, set
\[
\Delta:=\frac{2}{5\sqrt n},
\qquad
\Delta>0,
\qquad
\Delta^2=\frac{4}{25n}.
\]
Since \(n\ge1\), \(\Delta\le2/5\), so \(1/2+\Delta\le1\).  Define two endpoint binary laws \(P_0\) and \(P_1\) by uniform cell masses \(p_k=1/d\) and propensities \(\pi_k=1/2\) in every cell, with conditional response means
\[
\mu_{0k}(P_0)=\mu_{1k}(P_0)=\frac12,
\qquad
\mu_{0k}(P_1)=\frac12,
\qquad
\mu_{1k}(P_1)=\frac12+\Delta
\]
for every cell \(k\).  The inequalities \(0<\epsilon<1/2\) put \(\pi_k=1/2\) in \([\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\), and the preceding bound on \(\Delta\) puts all displayed conditional means in \([0,1]\).  Thus both laws have uniform cell masses, overlap at level \(\epsilon\), and a cell effect constant across cells.  Hence
\[
P_0,P_1\in\mathcal H_{n,d}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon),
\qquad
\psi(P_0)=0,
\qquad
\psi(P_1)=\Delta .
\]
For this endpoint pair, the Bernoulli \(\chi^2\) calculation gives total-variation distance at most \(1/2\) for the two \(n\)-fold product laws because
\[
n\,
\frac{(1/2)\Delta^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)}
=
\frac{8}{25}
\le \log 2 .
\]
Le Cam's two-point inequality therefore gives
\[
\frac{\Delta^2}{16}
\le
R_{n,d,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{bin}},
\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad
\frac{1}{100n}
\le
R_{n,d,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{bin}} .
\]
% lean: binaryExactMinimaxRisk_parametric_lower

Second, \cref{obj:lem:binary-exact-collision-lower}, applied with \(n\ge1\), \(d\ge1\), \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), and \(d\le n^2\), gives
\[
\frac{u_\epsilon^2}{64}\frac{d}{n^2}
\le
R_{n,d,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{bin}} .
\]
% lean: binaryExactMinimaxRisk_collision_lower

If \(d\le n\), then
\[
\frac{d}{n^2}\le \frac1n,
\qquad
\frac1n+\frac{d}{n^2}\le \frac2n .
\]
Using \(a_\epsilon\le1/200\), we get
\[
a_\epsilon\left(\frac1n+\frac{d}{n^2}\right)
\le
\frac1{200}\frac2n
=
\frac1{100n}
\le
R_{n,d,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{bin}} .
\]
% lean: zengBinaryExactHomogeneityLower

Otherwise \(n\le d\), and hence
\[
\frac1n\le \frac{d}{n^2},
\qquad
\frac1n+\frac{d}{n^2}\le 2\frac{d}{n^2}.
\]
Using \(a_\epsilon\le u_\epsilon^2/128\), we get
\[
a_\epsilon\left(\frac1n+\frac{d}{n^2}\right)
\le
\frac{u_\epsilon^2}{128}\,2\frac{d}{n^2}
=
\frac{u_\epsilon^2}{64}\frac{d}{n^2}
\le
R_{n,d,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{bin}} .
\]
% lean: zengBinaryExactHomogeneityLower
The two cases cover all \(d>0\), and the stated lower bound follows.
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:zeng-binary-exact-homogeneity-lower} records the binary exact-homogeneity lower scale associated with \citet{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}. The constants depend only on the fixed overlap level, and the active range \(d\le b_\epsilon n^2\) is the range transported and then saturated in the all-alphabet real-outcome statement.

The radius-channel converse uses a different binary source: a one-arm problem with arbitrary cell masses and zero control-arm response mean. Its minimax lower bound carries the large-alphabet polynomial scale that appears after the channel attenuation.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:zeng-binary-one-arm-lower}[Binary one-arm lower bound]
For every real overlap constant \(\epsilon\), if \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), then there exist constants \(a_{\epsilon},b_{\epsilon}>0\) and an integer \(N_{\epsilon}\) such that the following holds.  For every sample size \(n\) and alphabet size \(d\),
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Positive alphabet.)} \(d\ge 1\).
\item \textbf{(Large sample.)} \(n\ge N_{\epsilon}\).
\item \textbf{(Source range.)} \(d\le b_{\epsilon}n\log n\).
\end{itemize}
In the fixed-sample binary observed-data experiment with observations \((X,A,Y)\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\), arbitrary cell masses \(p_k=P(X=k)\), product sampling law \(P^{\otimes n}\), overlap
\[
\epsilon\le P(A=1\mid X=k)\le 1-\epsilon
\]
on every positive-mass category, and identically zero control-arm response mean \(E_P[Y\mid A=0,X=k]=0\), the treated-arm minimax risk satisfies
\[
\inf_{\widehat\psi}
\sup_P
E_{P^{\otimes n}}\left[
\left\{\widehat\psi-\sum_{k=1}^d p_k E_P[Y\mid A=1,X=k]\right\}^2
\right]
\ge
a_{\epsilon}
\left\{
\frac{1}{n}
+
\frac{d^2}{n^2(\log n)^2}
\right\},
\]
where the infimum is over measurable estimators of the fixed sample.
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:zeng-binary-one-arm-lower}]
Fix an overlap parameter \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).  For the binary control-zero one-arm experiment, let \(\mathcal C_{d,\epsilon}^{0}\) denote the class of product-source laws with \(X\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\), \(A,Y\in\{0,1\}\), arbitrary cell masses \(p_k=P(X=k)\), overlap
\[
\epsilon\le P(A=1\mid X=k)\le 1-\epsilon
\]
on every positive-mass cell, and
\[
E_P[Y\mid A=0,X=k]=0
\]
on the control arm.  Define the treated target and its minimax risk by
\[
\psi(P):=\sum_{k=1}^d p_k E_P[Y\mid A=1,X=k],
\]
and
\[
\mathcal R^{(1)}_{n,d,\epsilon}
:=
\inf_{\widehat\psi}
\sup_{P\in\mathcal C_{d,\epsilon}^{0}}
E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[
\{\widehat\psi-\psi(P)\}^2
\right].
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.oneArmMinimaxRisk

Set
\[
\kappa_\epsilon
:=
\min\left\{1,\frac{1-2\epsilon}{2\epsilon}\right\}.
\]
Then \(0<\kappa_\epsilon\le1\), and the shifted propensity construction remains inside the upper overlap endpoint because
\[
\epsilon(1+\kappa_\epsilon)\le 1-\epsilon .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.oneArmOverlapShiftRatio_pos
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.oneArmOverlapShiftRatio_le_one
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.epsilon_mul_one_add_overlapShiftRatio_le

Define
\[
A_\epsilon
:=
\frac{\kappa_\epsilon^{14}}
{4\cdot25600\cdot(153600000000000)^2},
\qquad
K_\epsilon
:=
\frac{10^{50}}{\kappa_\epsilon^{14}} .
\]
Both constants are positive.  Since \((\log n)^4=o(n)\), there is an integer \(N_0\) such that, for every \(n\ge N_0\),
\[
400\le n,
\qquad
(\log n)^4
\le
\frac{\kappa_\epsilon^{14}}{10^{50}}\,n .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.zengOneArmMinimaxLower

For every \(q\ge1\), \(n\ge N_0\), and
\[
K_\epsilon(\log n)^4\le q\le n\log n ,
\]
the shifted finite-grid construction gives the high-dimensional one-arm bound
\[
A_\epsilon
\frac{q^2}{n^2(\log n)^2}
\le
\mathcal R^{(1)}_{n,q,\epsilon}.
\]
This is the one-arm form of the discrete-adjustment lower bound of \citet{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}, using their shifted finite-grid prior construction.  The threshold implies \(q\ge2\).  Put \(m=q-1\), and define the logarithmic degree
\[
D_n:=\left\lceil\frac{8\log n}{\log 2}\right\rceil .
\]
Then
\[
1\le D_n,
\qquad
\frac{8\log n}{\log 2}\le D_n\le 20\log n
\qquad(n\ge 400).
\]
The comparison \(8\log n/\log 2\le D_n\) is the ceiling inequality.  The active-mass budget uses the consequence \(8\log n\le D_n\), obtained from \(\log 2\le1\).  The upper comparison uses \(D_n<8\log n/\log2+1\), \(1/2<\log2\), and \(1<\log n\) for \(n\ge400\):
\[
D_n
<
\frac{8\log n}{\log2}+1
\le
16\log n+1
\le
20\log n .
\]

Write
\[
a_D:=\frac{\kappa_\epsilon^4}{10000000000D_n^2},
\qquad
b_D:=\kappa_\epsilon a_D
      =\frac{\kappa_\epsilon^5}{10000000000D_n^2},
\qquad
s_n:=\frac{D_n}{128n}.
\]
Let \(c_{2D_n,j}:=-\cos(\pi j/(2D_n))\).  The shifted selected grid consists of \(2D_n+4\) positive nodes
\[
x_i=(i+1)a_D\quad(i=0,1,2),
\qquad
x_{j+3}=\frac{1+a_D}{2}+\frac{1-a_D}{2}c_{2D_n,j}
\quad(0\le j\le2D_n).
\]
For a probability mass \(\omega\) on these nodes, its inverse-size tilt on
\(\{\varnothing\}\cup\{0,\ldots,2D_n+3\}\) is
\[
\nu(\varnothing)=1-\sum_i \omega_i\frac{b_D}{x_i},
\qquad
\nu(i)=\omega_i\frac{b_D}{x_i}.
\]
The atom maps used in each active coordinate are the unnormalized mass atom
\[
\rho(\varnothing)=0,
\qquad
\rho(i)=s_nx_i,
\]
the propensity atom
\[
\pi(\varnothing)=\epsilon,
\qquad
\pi(i)=\epsilon\left(1+\frac{b_D}{x_i}\right),
\]
and the treated-mean atom
\[
\mu(\varnothing)=1,
\qquad
\mu(i)=\frac{x_i}{x_i+b_D}.
\]
Here \(\varnothing\) names the zero-mass active-coordinate atom; the residual anchor introduced below is a separate cell with treated mean zero.  The grid bounds give \(\rho(u)\in[0,s_n]\), \(\pi(u)\in[\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\), and \(\mu(u)\in[0,1]\) for every atom \(u\).  The construction chooses two signed-Jordan sides and tilts their positive and negative parts as above.  The resulting probability laws \(\nu_0,\nu_1\) satisfy, for every \(r,s,t\in\mathbb N\) with \(r+s+t\le D_n\), the raw observable triple-moment identities
\[
\sum_u \nu_0(u)\,
\rho(u)^r\{\rho(u)\pi(u)\}^s
\{\rho(u)\pi(u)\mu(u)\}^t
=
\sum_u \nu_1(u)\,
\rho(u)^r\{\rho(u)\pi(u)\}^s
\{\rho(u)\pi(u)\mu(u)\}^t .
\]
They also satisfy the common active-mass identity
\[
\int \rho(u)\,d\nu_0(u)=\int \rho(u)\,d\nu_1(u)=s_nb_D,
\]
and the functional separation
\[
s_nb_D\,
\frac{2\kappa_\epsilon^2}
{10(1+\kappa_\epsilon)(2+\kappa_\epsilon)(3+\kappa_\epsilon)}
\le
\int \rho(u)\mu(u)\,d\nu_0(u)-
\int \rho(u)\mu(u)\,d\nu_1(u).
\]

Set
\[
\Delta_{n,m}
=
m s_n b_D\,
\frac{2\kappa_\epsilon^2}
{10(1+\kappa_\epsilon)(2+\kappa_\epsilon)(3+\kappa_\epsilon)},
\qquad
\bar p:=m s_n b_D,
\qquad
\delta:=\Delta_{n,m}/16,
\]
and
\[
\theta_j:=m\int \rho(u)\mu(u)\,d\nu_j(u)
\qquad(j=0,1).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\Delta_{n,m}
=
m\frac{D_n}{128n}\,
\frac{\kappa_\epsilon^5}{10000000000D_n^2}\,
\frac{2\kappa_\epsilon^2}{10(1+\kappa_\epsilon)(2+\kappa_\epsilon)(3+\kappa_\epsilon)}.
\]
The scalar budgets verified by the construction are
\[
m s_n b_D\le1,
\qquad
\frac{2m}{n^4}\le\frac18,
\]
\[
2\,\frac{m s_n^2}{(\Delta_{n,m}/16)^2}\le\frac1{32},
\qquad
(ms_n)^2n^{-2}\le\frac{\Delta_{n,m}^2}{32}.
\]
The first budget follows from \(m\le q\le n\log n\), \(\kappa_\epsilon^5\le1\), and \(8\log n\le D_n\):
\[
m s_n b_D
=\frac{m\kappa_\epsilon^5}{128\cdot10000000000\,nD_n}
\le
\frac{n\log n}{128\cdot10000000000\,nD_n}
\le1.
\]
The raw-TV budget follows from \(m\le n\log n\), \(\log n\le n-1\), and \(n\ge400\), hence \(16\le n^2\):
\[
\frac{2m}{n^4}
\le
\frac{2n\log n}{n^4}
\le
\frac{2n^2}{n^4}
\le
\frac18 .
\]
For the good-event budget, the pole-gap lower bound
\[
\frac{\kappa_\epsilon^7}{1200000000000D_n^2}
\le
b_D\,
\frac{2\kappa_\epsilon^2}
{10(1+\kappa_\epsilon)(2+\kappa_\epsilon)(3+\kappa_\epsilon)}
\]
comes from
\[
\frac{\kappa_\epsilon^2}{120}
\le
\frac{2\kappa_\epsilon^2}
{10(1+\kappa_\epsilon)(2+\kappa_\epsilon)(3+\kappa_\epsilon)}.
\]
Together with \(D_n\le20\log n\) and
\(m\ge q/2\ge 10^{50}\kappa_\epsilon^{-14}(\log n)^4/2\), this gives
\[
m\left(
b_D\,
\frac{2\kappa_\epsilon^2}
{10(1+\kappa_\epsilon)(2+\kappa_\epsilon)(3+\kappa_\epsilon)}
\right)^2
\ge
\frac{10^{50}}{2(1200000000000)^2 20^4}
\ge
2\cdot16^2\cdot32.
\]
Since \(\Delta_{n,m}=ms_n\) times the pole-gap factor, this is exactly
\[
2\,\frac{m s_n^2}{(\Delta_{n,m}/16)^2}\le\frac1{32}.
\]
For the Poisson-tail budget, the same pole-gap lower bound and \(D_n\le20\log n\), combined with
\((\log n)^4\le\kappa_\epsilon^{14}10^{-50}n\), yield
\[
n
\left(
a_D\kappa_\epsilon\frac{2\kappa_\epsilon^2}
{10(1+\kappa_\epsilon)(2+\kappa_\epsilon)(3+\kappa_\epsilon)}
\right)^2
\ge
\frac{10^{50}}{(1200000000000)^2 20^4}
\ge32.
\]
Because \(n\ge1\), this implies \(32\le n^2\) times the squared pole-gap factor, which is equivalent to
\[
(ms_n)^2n^{-2}\le\frac{\Delta_{n,m}^2}{32}.
\]

Let \(\omega_j=\nu_j^{\otimes m}\) be the product prior on active-grid configurations \(z=(z_1,\ldots,z_m)\).  The good event for side \(j\) is
\[
G_j
:=
\left\{z:
0<1-\bar p+\sum_{i=1}^m \rho(z_i),
\left|(1-\bar p)+\sum_{i=1}^m \rho(z_i)-1\right|\le\delta,
\ \left|\sum_{i=1}^m \rho(z_i)\mu(z_i)-\theta_j\right|\le\delta
\right\}.
\]
The first scalar budget gives \(0\le1-\bar p\), so every good configuration normalizes to a control-zero law on \(m+1=q\) cells: the anchor has unnormalized mass \(1-\bar p\), propensity \(\epsilon\), and treated mean zero, while active coordinate \(i\) has unnormalized mass \(\rho(z_i)\), propensity \(\pi(z_i)\), and treated mean \(\mu(z_i)\).  The normalizing denominator is
\[
S_z:=1-\bar p+\sum_{i=1}^m \rho(z_i)>0.
\]
The two-statistic Chebyshev envelope for \(\rho(U)\) and \(\rho(U)\mu(U)\), each bounded by \(s_n\), and the good-event scalar budget give, for each \(j=0,1\),
\[
\omega_j(G_j^c)
\le
2\frac{m s_n^2}{\delta^2}
\le\frac1{32},
\qquad
\omega_j(G_j)>0.
\]
Condition on \(G_j\), with weights \(w_j(z)=\omega_j(z)/\omega_j(G_j)\) for \(z\in G_j\), and let \(P_j(z)\) be the normalized anchored law just described.  The good-event inequalities and the normalization bound give
\[
\left|\psi(P_j(z))-\theta_j\right|
\le 2\delta=\Delta_{n,m}/8
\qquad(z\in G_j),
\]
and the shifted-grid Jordan separation gives
\[
\Delta_{n,m}
\le |\theta_0-\theta_1|.
\]

For a configuration \(z\), define the product triple-count law \(K_z\) on \(\{0,\ldots,m\}\times\{0,1,2\}\) as follows.  The anchor coordinate has independent Poisson means
\[
0,
\qquad
4n(1-\bar p)\epsilon,
\qquad
4n(1-\bar p)(1-\epsilon),
\]
corresponding to treated successes, treated failures, and controls.  Active coordinate \(i\) has independent Poisson means
\[
4n \rho(z_i)\pi(z_i)\mu(z_i),
\qquad
4n \rho(z_i)\pi(z_i)\{1-\mu(z_i)\},
\qquad
4n \rho(z_i)\{1-\pi(z_i)\}.
\]
Thus \(K_z\) is the product, over the anchor and active coordinates, of these three-coordinate Poisson laws.  Write
\[
\mathsf Q_j^G:=\sum_{z\in G_j} w_j(z)K_z,
\qquad
\mathsf Q_j:=\sum_z \omega_j(z)K_z.
\]
For every atom \(u\), set \(x_u=0\) when \(u=\varnothing\) and \(x_u=x_i\) when \(u=i\); then \(x_u\in[0,1]\) and \(\rho(u)=s_nx_u\).  The logarithmic degree choice gives the Taylor budget
\[
4\{(4n)\rho(u)\}+2\log(n^2)
=
4\left\{(4n)\frac{D_n}{128n}x_u\right\}+2\log(n^2)
\le
(D_n+1)\log 2
\qquad\text{for every atom }u.
\]
Together with the displayed raw triple-moment identities through total degree \(D_n\), this budget gives the unconditioned predictive comparison
\[
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)
\le
\frac{2m}{n^4}
\le\frac18.
\]
Combining this with the two discarded-mass bounds gives the conditioned-mixture bound
\[
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0^G,\mathsf Q_1^G)
\le
\omega_0(G_0^c)+\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)+\omega_1(G_1^c)
\le
\frac1{32}+\frac18+\frac1{32}
\le\frac14.
\]
For every \(z\in G_j\), the observed triple label records treated success, treated failure, and control.  Under the Poissonized experiment with total mean \(4nS_z\) and normalized law \(P_j(z)\), the pushed-forward histogram of these labels is exactly \(K_z\); after currying the cell/triple array, the conditioned mixtures above are the actual conditioned Poisson histogram predictives for the laws \(P_j(z)\).

The active-mass budget and the bound
\[
\frac{2\kappa_\epsilon^2}{10(1+\kappa_\epsilon)(2+\kappa_\epsilon)(3+\kappa_\epsilon)}
\le 1
\]
give \(\Delta_{n,m}\le1\), so \(\delta=\Delta_{n,m}/16\le1/2\).  Therefore, for \(z\in G_j\),
\[
\frac12
\le
1-\bar p+\sum_{i=1}^m \rho(z_i)=S_z,
\]
and the Poisson mean \(4nS_z\) is at least \(2n\).  Also \(|\theta_j|\le ms_n\), because \(0\le \rho(u)\mu(u)\le s_n\).  The Poisson lower-tail bound at mean at least \(2n\), together with the fourth scalar budget, gives
\[
\theta_j^2\Pr\{\operatorname{Pois}(4nS_z)<n\}
\le
(ms_n)^2 n^{-2}
\le
\frac{\Delta_{n,m}^2}{32}
\qquad(z\in G_j,
\ j=0,1).
\]
With radius \(\Delta_{n,m}/8\), testing threshold \(\Delta_{n,m}/2\), conditioned total variation at most \(1/4\), and tail penalty \(\Delta_{n,m}^2/32\), the conditioned two-prior reduction yields
\[
\frac{
(\Delta_{n,m}/2)^2\{(1-1/4)/2\}
-2(\Delta_{n,m}/8)^2
-\Delta_{n,m}^2/32}{2}
\le
\mathcal R^{(1)}_{n,q,\epsilon},
\]
hence \(\Delta_{n,m}^2/64\le\mathcal R^{(1)}_{n,q,\epsilon}\).  The calibrated one-coordinate signal bound
\[
\frac{\kappa_\epsilon^7}{153600000000000\,nD_n}
\le
s_nb_D\,
\frac{2\kappa_\epsilon^2}
{10(1+\kappa_\epsilon)(2+\kappa_\epsilon)(3+\kappa_\epsilon)}
\]
and \(D_n\le20\log n\) convert this separation bound into the displayed
\(A_\epsilon q^2/\{n^2(\log n)^2\}\) lower bound, with the factor \(1/4\) in \(A_\epsilon\) supplied by \(q/2\le m=q-1\).
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.oneArmHighDimensional_shiftedPrior
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.oneArmCalibratedSignal_lower
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.oneArmHighDimensional_rate_of_calibrated_signal

The scalar assembly combines this high-dimensional term with the parametric one-arm lower bound
\[
\frac{1}{100n}\le \mathcal R^{(1)}_{n,d,\epsilon}
\]
valid for all \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge1\).  Choose \(N_\epsilon\ge\max\{N_0,3\}\) so that also
\[
(\log n)^6\le \frac{n}{K_\epsilon^2+1}
\qquad(n\ge N_\epsilon),
\]
and set
\[
a_\epsilon:=\min\left\{\frac1{200},\frac{A_\epsilon}{2}\right\},
\qquad
b_\epsilon:=1 .
\]
Then \(a_\epsilon>0\) and \(b_\epsilon>0\).  If
\(K_\epsilon(\log n)^4\le d\), the high-dimensional bound gives the rare-cell term and the parametric bound gives the \(1/n\) term; because
\[
a_\epsilon\frac1n\le\frac12\frac1{100n},
\qquad
a_\epsilon\frac{d^2}{n^2(\log n)^2}
\le
\frac12 A_\epsilon\frac{d^2}{n^2(\log n)^2},
\]
their sum is at most \(\mathcal R^{(1)}_{n,d,\epsilon}\).  If
\(d<K_\epsilon(\log n)^4\), then \(d^2\le K_\epsilon^2(\log n)^8\), and the logarithmic choice of \(N_\epsilon\) gives
\[
K_\epsilon^2(\log n)^6\le n.
\]
Therefore
\[
d^2
\le
K_\epsilon^2(\log n)^8
=
K_\epsilon^2(\log n)^6(\log n)^2
\le
n(\log n)^2,
\]
so that
\[
\frac{d^2}{n^2(\log n)^2}\le\frac1n.
\]
Consequently,
\[
a_\epsilon\left\{\frac1n+
\frac{d^2}{n^2(\log n)^2}\right\}
\le
\frac1{100n}
\le
\mathcal R^{(1)}_{n,d,\epsilon}.
\]
Hence, for every \(n\ge N_\epsilon\), \(d\ge1\), and \(d\le b_\epsilon n\log n\),
\[
a_\epsilon\left\{\frac1n+
\frac{d^2}{n^2(\log n)^2}\right\}
\le
\mathcal R^{(1)}_{n,d,\epsilon}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.oneArm_parametric_lower
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.zengOneArmMinimaxLower_of_highDimensional

Substituting the displayed definition of \(\mathcal R^{(1)}_{n,d,\epsilon}\) gives the asserted fixed-sample binary observed-data lower bound, with the same constants \(a_\epsilon,b_\epsilon,N_\epsilon\).
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteHeterogeneityFrontier.zengOneArmMinimaxLower
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:zeng-binary-one-arm-lower} formalizes the arbitrary-mass binary rare-cell lower scale associated with \citet{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}. In the main lower-bound construction, the hypothesis-independent channel in \cref{obj:def:least-favorable-handle} attenuates this binary separation by the radius factor before the affine scaling by \(M\).

The final source lemma is an occupancy reciprocal bound. It controls the event that no empirically crossed cell appears and the reciprocal of the crossed-cell count used by the collision estimator.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:zeng-usable-occupancy-reciprocal}[Usable occupancy reciprocal]
For every overlap parameter \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\), there are constants
\(b_{\epsilon},B_{\epsilon}>0\), depending only on \(\epsilon\), such that the following holds.
For every \(n,d\in\mathbb N\), every \(M,\sigma\in\mathbb R\), and every
\(P\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\), let \(Q_P^{(n)}\) denote the \(n\)-fold observed-data law and define
\[
N_{ak}:=\sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf 1\{X_i=k,A_i=a\},\qquad
N_k:=N_{0k}+N_{1k},\qquad
I_k:=\mathbf 1\{N_{0k}>0,\ N_{1k}>0\},
\]
with
\[
R_n:=\sum_{k=1}^d N_k I_k .
\]
Then
\[
Q_P^{(n)}(R_n=0)
\le
2\exp\left\{-b_{\epsilon}\frac{n^2}{n\vee d}\right\},
\]
and
\[
E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\left[
\mathbf 1\{R_n>0\}\frac{1}{R_n}
\right]
\le
B_{\epsilon}\left\{
\frac{n\vee d}{n^2}
+
\exp\left(-b_{\epsilon}\frac{n^2}{n\vee d}\right)
\right\}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:zeng-usable-occupancy-reciprocal}]
Set
\[
c_\epsilon:=
\min\left\{
\frac{\epsilon^2(1-e^{-2})\exp(-4/\epsilon)}{4},\frac{\epsilon}{4}
\right\}.
\]
For the active range \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), this constant is positive.  Choose
\[
b_\epsilon:=
\begin{cases}
c_\epsilon/8, & 0<\epsilon<1/2,\\
1, & \text{otherwise},
\end{cases}
\qquad
B_\epsilon:=
\begin{cases}
\max\{64/c_\epsilon,2\}, & 0<\epsilon<1/2,\\
1, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
Thus \(b_\epsilon>0\) and \(B_\epsilon>0\).  Fix \(n,d,M,\sigma\) and
\(P\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\).  By
\cref{obj:def:model-class}, \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), so the active branch of the
constants applies.
% lean: zengUsableOccupancyReciprocal

The case \(d=0\) is vacuous, since the cell masses of a probability law sum to
one while the sum over \(\{1,\ldots,d\}\) is empty.  For the endpoint \(n=0\),
all empirical counts are zero and every ratio in the displayed bound is assigned
the value zero when its denominator is zero.  Then \(R_0=0\) almost surely,
\[
Q_P^{(0)}(R_0=0)\le 1\le 2,
\qquad
E_{Q_P^{(0)}}\!\left[\mathbf 1\{R_0>0\}R_0^{-1}\right]=0.
\]
In this branch \(d>0\), the exponential term in the reciprocal bound is one
and the division term \((0\vee d)/0^2\) is zero under the same convention; hence
the reciprocal bound follows from \(B_\epsilon>0\).
% lean: zengUsableOccupancyReciprocal

Assume now that \(n>0\) and \(d>0\).  Put
\[
\kappa_{n,d}:=c_\epsilon\frac{(n/2)^2}{\max\{n/2,d\}},
\qquad
s_{n,d}:=\frac{n^2}{n\vee d}.
\]
Since \(n/2\le n\),
\[
\frac{c_\epsilon}{4}s_{n,d}\le \kappa_{n,d}.
\]
% lean: zengUsableOccupancyReciprocal

Let \(P_O\) be the one-observation law from
\cref{obj:def:sample-experiment}, and let \(\bar P\) be its marginal law for the
cell-arm mark \((X,A)\).  Under the auxiliary marked-Poisson law
\(\Pi^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}\), attach the deterministic auxiliary mark \(0\) and let
the arm-cell counts \(N^{\mathrm P}_{ak}\) be independent Poisson variables
with means \((n/2)\bar P\{(k,a)\}\).  Define
\[
R^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}
:=
\sum_{k=1}^d
\bigl(N^{\mathrm P}_{0k}+N^{\mathrm P}_{1k}\bigr)
\mathbf 1\{N^{\mathrm P}_{0k}>0,\ N^{\mathrm P}_{1k}>0\}.
\]
The Poisson Laplace estimate used here is the following consequence of the
explicit constant \(c_\epsilon\).  If independent two-arm Poisson counts in cell
\(k\) have means \(\mu_{0k},\mu_{1k}\), if
\(x_k=\mu_{0k}+\mu_{1k}\), \(\sum_k x_k=t\), and
\(\mu_{ak}\ge\epsilon x_k\) for both arms, then
\[
E\exp\!\left\{-\sum_{k=1}^d
(Z_{0k}+Z_{1k})\mathbf 1\{Z_{0k}>0,\ Z_{1k}>0\}\right\}
\le
\exp\!\left\{-c_\epsilon\frac{t^2}{\max\{t,d\}}\right\}.
\]
Indeed, for one cell with total intensity \(x\), retaining the \((1,1)\) atom
on \(x\le4/\epsilon\) gives the quadratic bound
\(\exp\{-\epsilon^2(1-e^{-2})e^{-4/\epsilon}x^2\}\), while the elementary
three-term envelope on \(x>4/\epsilon\) gives the linear bound
\(\exp\{-\epsilon x/2\}\).  Multiplication over cells gives the exponent
\[
\epsilon^2(1-e^{-2})e^{-4/\epsilon}
\sum_{x_k\le4/\epsilon}x_k^2
+
\frac{\epsilon}{2}\sum_{x_k>4/\epsilon}x_k.
\]
This exponent is at least \(c_\epsilon t^2/\max\{t,d\}\): use Cauchy--Schwarz
on the light cells when they carry more than half of \(t\), and otherwise use
the heavy-cell mass.  The overlap condition in \cref{obj:def:model-class}
supplies \(\mu_{ak}\ge\epsilon x_k\) for the present arm-cell intensities,
trivially also on zero-mass cells, with \(t=n/2\).  Therefore
\[
E_{\Pi^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}}\exp\{-R^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}\}\le e^{-\kappa_{n,d}}.
\]
Because \(\mathbf 1\{R^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}=0\}\le
\exp\{-R^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}\}\),
\[
\Pi^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}(R^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}=0)\le e^{-\kappa_{n,d}}.
\]
Also, since \(\kappa_{n,d}>0\), every integer \(r\ge0\) satisfies
\[
\mathbf 1\{r=0\}+\mathbf 1\{r>0\}\frac1r
\le
\frac{2}{\kappa_{n,d}}+e^{\kappa_{n,d}/2}e^{-r}.
\]
Applying this pointwise bound to \(R^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}\) and using the Laplace
bound yields
\[
E_{\Pi^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}}\!\left[
\mathbf 1\{R^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}=0\}
+\mathbf 1\{R^{\mathrm P}_{n/2}>0\}(R^{\mathrm P}_{n/2})^{-1}\right]
\le
\frac{2}{\kappa_{n,d}}+e^{-\kappa_{n,d}/2}.
\]
% lean: markedPoisson_zero_penalty_bounds

On an infinite i.i.d. stream of cell-arm marks, usable occupancy is increasing
with the prefix length.  Hence the zero indicator and the combined
zero-reciprocal penalty are antitone in the prefix length.  Since a
\(\operatorname{Pois}(n/2)\) count is at most \(n\) with probability at least
\(1/2\), half-intensity de-Poissonization, together with the transport from the
cell-arm stream back to the fixed observed-data product law, gives
\[
Q_P^{(n)}(R_n=0)\le 2e^{-\kappa_{n,d}}.
\]
Applying the same transfer to the combined penalty and using
\(
\mathbf 1\{R_n>0\}R_n^{-1}\le
\mathbf 1\{R_n=0\}+\mathbf 1\{R_n>0\}R_n^{-1}
\)
gives
\[
E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[\mathbf 1\{R_n>0\}R_n^{-1}\right]
\le
2\left(\frac{2}{\kappa_{n,d}}+e^{-\kappa_{n,d}/2}\right).
\]
% lean: augmented_half_depoissonization

Using \(\kappa_{n,d}\ge(c_\epsilon/4)s_{n,d}\) and \(b_\epsilon=c_\epsilon/8\),
\[
2e^{-\kappa_{n,d}}\le 2\exp\{-b_\epsilon s_{n,d}\}.
\]
% lean: zengUsableOccupancyReciprocal
Moreover,
\[
\frac{4}{\kappa_{n,d}}
\le
\frac{64}{c_\epsilon}\frac{n\vee d}{n^2},
\qquad
2e^{-\kappa_{n,d}/2}\le 2\exp\{-b_\epsilon s_{n,d}\}.
\]
Since \(B_\epsilon\ge64/c_\epsilon\) and \(B_\epsilon\ge2\),
\[
E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[\mathbf 1\{R_n>0\}R_n^{-1}\right]
\le
B_\epsilon\left\{
\frac{n\vee d}{n^2}
+
\exp\left(-b_\epsilon\frac{n^2}{n\vee d}\right)
\right\}.
\]
% lean: zengUsableOccupancyReciprocal
This proves both bounds.
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:zeng-usable-occupancy-reciprocal} records the occupancy statement associated with the collision calculations of \citet{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}. The two displayed inequalities bound, respectively, the zero-crossing probability and the expected reciprocal occupancy on the crossed-cell event. These controls are the probabilistic ingredients behind the collision estimator's dependence on \(d/n^2\) and fixed overlap.


\section{Proofs for upper bounds}

This appendix records the auxiliary inequalities that support the constructive upper bounds in \cref{obj:thm:robust-upper-construction-resolution-all-d,obj:thm:frontier-upper-all-d}. The proof has two mechanisms. The polynomial branch first controls the shifted-Chebyshev coefficients, then bounds same-cell and distinct-cell factorial covariances, then combines those bounds with a deterministic heavy-set plug-in argument. The collision branch controls the occupancy-weighted treated-control contrast on empirically crossed cells. These mechanisms correspond to the two estimator branches introduced in \cref{obj:def:polynomial-handle,obj:def:collision-handle}.

The first ingredient is a coefficient envelope for the shifted-Chebyshev polynomial used in the light-cell component of \cref{obj:def:polynomial-handle}. It converts the polynomial coefficients into a scale bound on cell masses below the light-cell threshold, matching the large-alphabet approximation strategy of \citet{JiaoVenkatHanWeissman2015,WuYang2016Entropy}.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:shifted-chebyshev-coefficient-envelope}[Shifted coefficient envelope]
Let \(K\) be a positive integer, let \(B,p\in\mathbb R\), and define, for \(0\le j\le K-2\),
\[
g_{Kj}
:=
(-1)^j
\frac{2^{2j+3}}{K(K+j+2)}
\binom{K+j+2}{2j+4}.
\]
Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Positive scale.)} \(B>0\).
\item \textbf{(Nonnegative mass.)} \(p\ge 0\).
\item \textbf{(Budget bound.)} \(p\le B\).
\end{itemize}
Then
\[
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}
\left|\frac{g_{Kj}}{B^{j+1}}\right|p^{j+2}
\le
B\,6^K.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:shifted-chebyshev-coefficient-envelope}]
Define the absolute coefficient envelope
\[
\mathcal G_K(x)
:=
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}|g_{Kj}|x^j .
\]
Let \(T_K\) be the Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind. The shifted expansion gives
\[
T_K(1-2x)
=
1-2K^2x
+
2K^2x^2
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2} g_{Kj}x^j .
\] % lean: chebyshev_gPolynomial_identity
This follows by separating the linear term in the standard shifted-Chebyshev expansion
\[
T_K(1-2x)
=
1+
\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}
(-1)^\ell
\frac{K}{K+\ell}
\binom{K+\ell}{2\ell}
4^\ell x^\ell
\]
and reindexing the terms \(\ell=j+2\), whose coefficients are \(2K^2g_{Kj}\). % lean: shifted_chebyshev_expansion

Since the sign of \(g_{Kj}\) is \((-1)^j\), one has
\[
|g_{Kj}|=(-1)^jg_{Kj},
\qquad
\mathcal G_K(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}(-z)^j .
\] % lean: sign_gCoefficient
Putting \(x=-1\) in the shifted identity therefore yields
\[
T_K(3)=1+2K^2+2K^2\mathcal G_K(1).
\] % lean: gpos_eq
Because \(\mathcal G_K(1)\ge0\) and \(K\ge1\),
\[
\mathcal G_K(1)\le T_K(3).
\] % lean: gpos_nonneg
The Chebyshev recurrence \(T_{n+2}(3)=6T_{n+1}(3)-T_n(3)\), with the initial cases
\(T_0(3)=1\) and \(T_1(3)=3\), gives
\[
T_K(3)\le 6^K .
\] % lean: chebyshev_three_le
If \(0\le x\le1\), then termwise monotonicity gives
\[
\mathcal G_K(x)
=
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}|g_{Kj}|x^j
\le
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}|g_{Kj}|
=
\mathcal G_K(1)
\le 6^K .
\] % lean: shiftedCoefficientEnvelope_le
% lean: shiftedCoefficientEnvelope_le_six_pow

Now set
\[
x:=\frac pB .
\]
The assumptions \(B>0\), \(p\ge0\), and \(p\le B\) imply
\[
0\le x\le1 .
\] % lean: shiftedCoefficient_weighted_sum_le_of_le
For each \(j\), using \(B>0\),
\[
\left|\frac{g_{Kj}}{B^{j+1}}\right|p^{j+2}
=
\frac{p^2}{B}\,|g_{Kj}|
\left(\frac pB\right)^j .
\]
Summing this identity over \(j=0,\ldots,K-2\) gives
\[
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}
\left|\frac{g_{Kj}}{B^{j+1}}\right|p^{j+2}
=
\frac{p^2}{B}\,
\mathcal G_K\!\left(\frac pB\right).
\]
The envelope bound at \(x=p/B\) and the inequality \(p^2\le B^2\) give
\[
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}
\left|\frac{g_{Kj}}{B^{j+1}}\right|p^{j+2}
\le
\frac{p^2}{B}6^K
\le
B6^K .
\]
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:shifted-chebyshev-coefficient-envelope} gives the deterministic side of the light-cell calculation. Once a cell mass is below the approximation scale \(B\), the entire signed coefficient expansion is controlled by \(B6^K\), so the later variance bounds can aggregate the polynomial terms without tracking each coefficient separately.

The next two moment bounds handle the factorial statistics that estimate the light-cell polynomial terms. They are stated for an independent block of size \leanref{sym:m}{\ensuremath{m}} with product law \leanref{sym:Q_P^{(m)}}{\ensuremath{Q_P^{(m)}}}, the block analogue of the observed product experiment in \cref{obj:def:sample-experiment}. The same-cell bound includes the extra covariance contribution created when two statistics use the same adjustment cell, and the distinct-cell bound records the simpler interaction across different cells.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:same-cell-factorial-cross-moment}[Same-cell cross moment]
Let \(P\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) be as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, let \(k\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\), and write \(p_k=P(X=k)\) for the cell mass.  For a block of \(m\) independent observations with joint law \(Q_P^{(m)}\), write \(V_i=Y_i/M\).  For an arm \(c\in\{0,1\}\) and an order \(q\in\mathbb N\), define
\[
U_{k,c,q}
:=
\frac{1}{(m)_{q+2}}
\sum_{i_0,\ldots,i_{q+1}\ {\rm distinct}}
V_{i_0}\mathbf 1\{X_{i_0}=k,A_{i_0}=c\}
\mathbf 1\{X_{i_1}=k\}
\prod_{t=2}^{q+1}\mathbf 1\{X_{i_t}=k,A_{i_t}=c\}.
\]
Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(First order.)} \(j+2\le K\).
\item \textbf{(Second order.)} \(r+2\le K\).
\item \textbf{(Block size.)} \(4(K+2)^2\le m\).
\end{itemize}
Then, for all arms \(a,b\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\left|\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}\!\left(U_{k,a,j},U_{k,b,r}\right)\right|
\le
\frac{2(K+2)^2}{m}\,p_k^{j+2}p_k^{r+2}
+
\exp(1)\left(\frac54p_k\right)^{r+2}
\left(\frac54p_k+\frac{r+2}{m}\right)^{j+2}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:same-cell-factorial-cross-moment}]
Write
\[
p:=p_k=P(X=k).
\]
For an observed record \(O=(X,A,Y)\), write \(V=Y/M\).  Define the left coordinate factors
\[
L_q(O):=
\begin{cases}
V\mathbf 1\{X=k,A=a\}, & q=0,\\
\mathbf 1\{X=k\}, & q=1,\\
\mathbf 1\{X=k,A=a\}, & 2\le q\le j+1,
\end{cases}
\]
and define the right coordinate factors \(R_s\) in the same way, with \((b,r,s)\) in place of \((a,j,q)\).

For a partial matching \(N\) of size \(h\) between \(\{0,\ldots,j+1\}\) and \(\{0,\ldots,r+1\}\), let \(T_N\) be the merged coordinate set, and let \(\iota_L\) and \(\iota_R\) map left and right coordinates into \(T_N\). Thus \(\iota_R(s)=\iota_L(q)\) exactly when \(N\) matches \(q\) with \(s\). Put
\[
\gamma_N:=
\frac{(m)_{(j+2)+(r+2)-h}}{(m)_{j+2}(m)_{r+2}},
\]
\[
F_{N,t}(O):=
\prod_{q:\,\iota_L(q)=t}L_q(O)
\prod_{s:\,\iota_R(s)=t}R_s(O),
\qquad
M_N:=\int \prod_{t\in T_N}F_{N,t}(O_t)\,
  dP_O^{\otimes T_N}((O_t)_{t\in T_N}).
\]
Finally set
\[
m_{k,a,j}:=\int\prod_{q=0}^{j+1}L_q(O_q)\,
  dP_O^{\otimes(j+2)}((O_q)_q),
\qquad
m_{k,b,r}:=\int\prod_{s=0}^{r+1}R_s(O_s)\,
  dP_O^{\otimes(r+2)}((O_s)_s).
\]
The block-size condition implies \(K\le m\), hence \(j+2\le m\) and \(r+2\le m\). The selector factors are measurable bounded indicators, and the marked factor is integrable under the moment conditions in \cref{obj:def:model-class}; the merged kernels are finite products with at most two marked factors at any single observed coordinate, hence integrable. With the coordinate factors just defined, each statistic \(U_{k,a,j}\) and \(U_{k,b,r}\) is its normalized ordered-product sum over injective maps into the block. Expanding the product of the two normalized sums and grouping each ordered pair of injective maps by the partial matching \(N\) that records their shared block indices gives a contribution \(\gamma_N M_N\) for matching pattern \(N\). The empty matching has product moment \(m_{k,a,j}m_{k,b,r}\) by independence and is then centered by subtracting \(m_{k,a,j}m_{k,b,r}\). Therefore
\[
\operatorname{Cov}(U_{k,a,j},U_{k,b,r})
=
(\gamma_\varnothing-1)m_{k,a,j}m_{k,b,r}
+
\sum_{h=1}^{\min(j+2,r+2)}
\sum_{N\in\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)}
\gamma_N\,M_N,
\]
where \(\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)\) is the finite set of partial matchings of size \(h\).
% lean: centeredCrossMoment_allBlockOrderedMarkedFactorial

Each one-coordinate mean is bounded by the cell mass:
\[
\left|\int L_q(O)\,dP_O(O)\right|\le p,
\qquad
\left|\int R_s(O)\,dP_O(O)\right|\le p.
\]
For the outcome-marked coordinate, the argument splits according to the cell mass. If \(p=0\), the observed arm-cell event \(\{X=k,A=c\}\) has \(P_O\)-mass zero for each arm \(c\), so the marked integral is zero. If \(p>0\), the supported-cell branch of \cref{obj:ass:mean-normalization} applies to the arm-cell outcome law for each arm \(c\), and \cref{obj:ass:overlap} supplies positive observed arm-cell mass when this law is read as the observed conditional law. The mean bound and \(M\ge1\) from \cref{obj:def:model-class} give absolute normalized arm-cell mean at most \(1/2\). The observed arm-cell restriction has total mass
\(p\{\pi_k\mathbf 1_{\{c=1\}}+(1-\pi_k)\mathbf 1_{\{c=0\}}\}\le p\), so the absolute marked integral is bounded by \(p/2\). The selector-only coordinates integrate to cell or arm-cell probabilities bounded by \(p\). Since the product-law means factor coordinatewise,
\[
|m_{k,a,j}|\le p^{j+2},
\qquad
|m_{k,b,r}|\le p^{r+2}.
\]
The empty-matching normalization satisfies, under \(j+2\le K\), \(r+2\le K\), and \(4(K+2)^2\le m\),
\[
|\gamma_\varnothing-1|
\le
\frac{2(K+2)^2}{m}.
\]
Therefore
\[
|(\gamma_\varnothing-1)m_{k,a,j}m_{k,b,r}|
\le
\frac{2(K+2)^2}{m}p^{j+2}p^{r+2}.
\]
% lean: marked_emptyMatchingNormalization_sub_one_le
% lean: orderedProductMean_markedFactorialCoordinate_abs_le

It remains to bound the positive-matching part.  For each merged coordinate \(t\in T_N\), the fiber definition of \(F_{N,t}\) gives exactly one of the following forms: a single left factor \(L_q\), a single right factor \(R_s\), or a matched product \(L_qR_s\). The first two cases include the lone unmarked cell-indicator coordinate. For single factors, the preceding one-coordinate bound gives
\[
\left|\int L_q\,dP_O\right|\le p\le \frac54p,
\qquad
\left|\int R_s\,dP_O\right|\le p\le \frac54p.
\]
For matched products, first note the square-moment bounds
\[
\int L_q(O)^2\,dP_O(O)\le \frac54p,
\qquad
\int R_s(O)^2\,dP_O(O)\le \frac54p.
\]
For the outcome-marked coordinate these bounds again split on \(p\): the zero-mass branch is null on every arm-cell event, while on \(p>0\), \cref{obj:ass:overlap} supplies the positive arm-cell support for either arm. The arm-cell law then satisfies the mean and central second-moment bounds from \cref{obj:ass:mean-normalization,obj:ass:second-central-moment}; expanding the second moment around \(\mu_{ck}\) gives an unnormalized second moment at most \(5M^2/4\), hence, using \(M\ge1\) from \cref{obj:def:model-class}, a normalized second moment at most \(5/4\). Multiplying by the observed arm-cell mass, which is at most \(p\), gives the displayed square-moment bound for the marked coordinate. Selector coordinates are idempotent indicators with event mass at most \(p\). Hence, by integrability, \(|\int fg|\le\int|fg|\) and \(2|fg|\le f^2+g^2\),
\[
\left|\int L_q(O)R_s(O)\,dP_O(O)\right|
\le
\frac12\int\{L_q(O)^2+R_s(O)^2\}\,dP_O(O)
\le
\frac54p.
\]
Consequently every merged coordinate satisfies
\[
\left|
\int F_{N,t}(O)\,dP_O(O)
\right|
\le
\frac54p.
\]
% lean: mergedMarkedCoordinateFactor_integral_abs_le

The merged product moment factors over the merged coordinates:
\[
M_N=\prod_{t\in T_N}\int F_{N,t}(O)\,dP_O(O).
\]
Since \(|T_N|=(j+2)+(r+2)-h\),
\[
|M_N|
\le
\left(\frac54p\right)^{(j+2)+(r+2)-h}.
\]
% lean: mergedProductMoment_markedFactorialCoordinate_same_cell_abs_le

Using \(\gamma_N\ge0\) and the triangle inequality,
\[
\left|
\sum_{h=1}^{\min(j+2,r+2)}
\sum_{N\in\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)}
\gamma_NM_N
\right|
\le
\sum_{h=1}^{\min(j+2,r+2)}
\sum_{N\in\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)}
\gamma_N
\left(\frac54p\right)^{(j+2)+(r+2)-h}.
\]
It remains only to sum the normalization weights.  Let \(u=j+2\) and \(v=r+2\). For each \(h\), the size-\(h\) normalization bound and the partial-matching count give
\[
\sum_{N\in\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)}\gamma_N
\le
\binom{u}{h}v^h\frac{\exp(1)}{m^h}.
\]
Here \(|\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)|=\binom{u}{h}\binom{v}{h}h!\), and the displayed bound uses \(\binom{v}{h}\le v^h/h!\) together with the factorial-normalization estimate supplied by the block-size condition \(4(K+2)^2\le m\). Therefore, for every \(x\ge0\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\sum_{h=1}^{\min(u,v)}
\sum_{N\in\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)}
\gamma_N x^{u+v-h} \\
&\qquad\le
\sum_{h=1}^{\min(u,v)}
\exp(1)x^v\binom{u}{h}x^{u-h}\left(\frac{v}{m}\right)^h \\
&\qquad\le
\sum_{h=0}^{u}
\exp(1)x^v\binom{u}{h}x^{u-h}\left(\frac{v}{m}\right)^h
=
\exp(1)x^v\left(x+\frac{v}{m}\right)^u.
\end{aligned}
\]
The choice \(x=(5/4)p\) is admissible because \(p\ge0\), and it yields
\[
\left|
\sum_{h=1}^{\min(j+2,r+2)}
\sum_{N\in\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)}
\gamma_NM_N
\right|
\le
\exp(1)\left(\frac54p\right)^{r+2}
\left(\frac54p+\frac{r+2}{m}\right)^{j+2}.
\]
% lean: positiveMatchingNormalization_weighted_sum_le

Combining the empty-matching and positive-matching bounds by the triangle inequality gives
\[
\left|\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(U_{k,a,j},U_{k,b,r})\right|
\le
\frac{2(K+2)^2}{m}p_k^{j+2}p_k^{r+2}
+
\exp(1)\left(\frac54p_k\right)^{r+2}
\left(\frac54p_k+\frac{r+2}{m}\right)^{j+2},
\]
as claimed.
% lean: centeredCrossMoment_allBlockOrderedMarkedFactorial_same_cell_abs_le
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:distinct-cell-factorial-cross-moment}[Distinct-cell cross moment]
Let \(P\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) be a model in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, and let \(O_1,\ldots,O_m\) be an independent block with joint law \(Q_P^{(m)}\).  For \(k\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\), \(a\in\{0,1\}\), and \(j\ge 0\), let \(U_{k,a,j}\) be the ordered distinct-index one-mark factorial statistic from \cref{obj:lem:same-cell-factorial-cross-moment}, equivalently
\[
U_{k,a,j}
=
\frac{1}{(m)_{j+2}}
\sum_{\substack{i_0,\ldots,i_{j+1}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}\\ \text{all distinct}}}
\frac{Y_{i_0}}{M}
\mathbf 1\{X_{i_0}=k,A_{i_0}=a\}
\mathbf 1\{X_{i_1}=k\}
\prod_{q=2}^{j+1}\mathbf 1\{X_{i_q}=k,A_{i_q}=a\}.
\]
Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Distinct cells.)} \(k\ne \ell\).
\item \textbf{(Admissible orders.)} \(j+2\le K\) and \(r+2\le K\).
\item \textbf{(Block size.)} \(4(K+2)^2\le m\).
\end{itemize}
Then, for all arms \(a,b\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\left|
\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}\!\left(U_{k,a,j},U_{\ell,b,r}\right)
\right|
\le
\frac{2(K+2)^2}{m}\,
p_k^{j+2}p_{\ell}^{r+2}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:distinct-cell-factorial-cross-moment}]
Let \(P_O\) be the one-observation marginal of \(Q_P^{(m)}\), and for an observed record \(O=(X,A,Y)\) write \(V=Y/M\).  Define the left coordinate factors
\[
L_q(O):=
\begin{cases}
V\mathbf 1\{X=k,A=a\}, & q=0,\\
\mathbf 1\{X=k\}, & q=1,\\
\mathbf 1\{X=k,A=a\}, & 2\le q\le j+1,
\end{cases}
\]
and the right coordinate factors
\[
R_s(O):=
\begin{cases}
V\mathbf 1\{X=\ell,A=b\}, & s=0,\\
\mathbf 1\{X=\ell\}, & s=1,\\
\mathbf 1\{X=\ell,A=b\}, & 2\le s\le r+1.
\end{cases}
\]
Put
\[
\mu_{k,a,j}:=
\int \prod_{q=0}^{j+1}L_q(O_q)\,
  dP_O^{\otimes(j+2)}((O_q)_q),
\qquad
\mu_{\ell,b,r}:=
\int \prod_{s=0}^{r+1}R_s(O_s)\,
  dP_O^{\otimes(r+2)}((O_s)_s).
\]
For a partial matching \(N\) of size \(h\) between \(\{0,\ldots,j+1\}\) and \(\{0,\ldots,r+1\}\), let \(T_N\) be the merged coordinate set and let \(\iota_L,\iota_R\) send left and right coordinates into \(T_N\), with \(\iota_L(q)=\iota_R(s)\) exactly for matched pairs.  Define
\[
\mathcal M_{j,r}(h):=\{N: |N|=h\},
\qquad
\eta_N:=
\frac{(m)_{(j+2)+(r+2)-h}}{(m)_{j+2}(m)_{r+2}},
\]
and
\[
\Gamma_N:=
\int
\left\{\prod_{q=0}^{j+1}L_q(O_{\iota_L(q)})\right\}
\left\{\prod_{s=0}^{r+1}R_s(O_{\iota_R(s)})\right\}
\,dP_O^{\otimes T_N}((O_t)_{t\in T_N}).
\]
The ordered-factorial covariance expansion gives
\[
\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(U_{k,a,j},U_{\ell,b,r})
=
(\eta_{\varnothing}-1)\mu_{k,a,j}\mu_{\ell,b,r}
+
\sum_{h=1}^{\min(j+2,r+2)}
\sum_{N\in\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)}
\eta_N\Gamma_N .
\]
% lean: centeredCrossMoment_allBlockOrderedMarkedFactorial

Fix \(N\in\mathcal M_{j,r}(h)\) with \(h>0\).  Choose a matched pair \((q,s)\).  At the merged coordinate \(\iota_L(q)=\iota_R(s)\), the factor \(L_q\) contains \(\mathbf 1\{X=k\}\) and the factor \(R_s\) contains \(\mathbf 1\{X=\ell\}\).  Since \(k\ne\ell\), their product is zero pointwise:
\[
\mathbf 1\{X=k\}\mathbf 1\{X=\ell\}=0.
\]
Thus the merged kernel is identically zero and
\[
\Gamma_N=0
\qquad (h>0).
\]
All positive-size matching terms in the expansion therefore vanish. % lean: mergedProductMoment_markedFactorialCoordinate_eq_zero_of_cell_ne

It remains to bound the empty-matching term.  The admissible-order and block-size assumptions give
\[
|\eta_{\varnothing}-1|
\le
\frac{2(K+2)^2}{m}.
\]
For the product means, each one-coordinate mean is bounded by the corresponding cell mass:
\[
\left|\int L_q(O)\,dP_O(O)\right|\le p_k,
\qquad
\left|\int R_s(O)\,dP_O(O)\right|\le p_{\ell}.
\]
For the outcome-marked coordinate, if the relevant cell mass is zero then the arm-cell event is null and the marked integral is zero.  If the relevant cell \(x\) has positive mass, \cref{obj:ass:mean-normalization} and \(M\ge1\) from \cref{obj:def:model-class} give, for the corresponding arm \(c\),
\[
\left|\int y/M\,dP_{Y\mid A=c,X=x}(y)\right|\le \frac12,
\]
so multiplying by the arm-cell probability bounds the marked coordinate by one half of the cell mass.  The selector-only coordinates integrate to \(P_O(X=x)\) or \(P_O(X=x,A=c)\), both bounded by the cell mass.  Since the product-law means factor coordinatewise,
\[
|\mu_{k,a,j}|
\le p_k^{j+2},
\qquad
|\mu_{\ell,b,r}|
\le p_{\ell}^{r+2}.
\]
% lean: marked_emptyMatchingNormalization_sub_one_le
% lean: orderedProductMean_markedFactorialCoordinate_abs_le

Combining the vanished positive-size terms with the preceding bounds yields
\[
\left|\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(U_{k,a,j},U_{\ell,b,r})\right|
\le
\frac{2(K+2)^2}{m}\,
p_k^{j+2}p_{\ell}^{r+2},
\]
which is the claimed bound. % lean: centeredCrossMoment_allBlockOrderedMarkedFactorial_cell_ne_abs_le
\end{proof}

Together, \cref{obj:lem:same-cell-factorial-cross-moment,obj:lem:distinct-cell-factorial-cross-moment} supply the weak-moment covariance control needed for the continuous-outcome polynomial lift. The same-cell inequality carries the additional term that remains after the two factorial arrays overlap in a cell; across distinct cells, the block-size term is the relevant covariance contribution. These estimates use the ordered distinct-index structure familiar from symmetric-statistic calculations \citep{Hoeffding1948,Serfling1980}.

For the heavy-cell part of a fixed polynomial branch, the argument uses empirical arm-cell means with a total value when an arm-cell count is empty. The convention is local to the block calculation and agrees with the totalized plug-in convention used in \cref{obj:def:polynomial-handle}.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_6}[Empirical arm-cell mean \(\widehat\mu_{ak}\)]
For a block of observations \(O_i=(X_i,A_i,Y_i)\), define the arm-cell count and outcome total by \[N_{ak}:=\sum_i \mathbf 1\{X_i=k,A_i=a\},\qquad S_{ak}:=\sum_i \mathbf 1\{X_i=k,A_i=a\}Y_i .\] The empirical arm-cell mean is \[\widehat\mu_{ak}:=\begin{cases}S_{ak}/N_{ak},&N_{ak}>0,\\ 0,&N_{ak}=0.\end{cases}\]
\end{definitionv}

The next aggregate covariance bound combines the coefficient envelope with the same-cell and distinct-cell moment bounds. It is the main stochastic input for summing the light-cell polynomial contributions over a deterministic set.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:linear-mark-factorial-covariance}[Factorial covariance bound]
For every overlap parameter \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there is a constant \(C_{\epsilon}>0\) such that the following statement holds.  Let \(d,m\), and \(K\) be nonnegative integers, let \(M,\sigma,B\in\mathbb R\), let \(P\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, and let \(S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,d\}\) be deterministic.  For a block of \(m\) independent observations with joint law \(Q_P^{(m)}\), write \(V_i=Y_i/M\).  For the polynomial degree \(K\), define the shifted-Chebyshev coefficients
\[
g_{Kj}
:=
(-1)^j\frac{2^{2j+3}}{K(K+j+2)}
\binom{K+j+2}{2j+4},
\qquad 0\le j\le K-2.
\]
For \(0\le j\le K-2\), define
\[
U_{k,a,j}
:=
\frac{1}{(m)_{j+2}}
\sum_{i_0,\ldots,i_{j+1}\ {\rm distinct}}
V_{i_0}\mathbf 1\{X_{i_0}=k,A_{i_0}=a\}
\mathbf 1\{X_{i_1}=k\}
\prod_{t=2}^{j+1}\mathbf 1\{X_{i_t}=k,A_{i_t}=a\}.
\]
Set
\[
\widehat P_k
:=
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}
\frac{g_{Kj}}{B^{j+1}}
\{U_{k,1,j}-U_{k,0,j}\}.
\]
Assume that
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Degree.)} \(K\ge 2\).
\item \textbf{(Scale.)} \(B>0\).
\item \textbf{(Light cells.)} \(p_k\le B/4\) for every \(k\in S\).
\item \textbf{(Block size.)} \(4(K+2)^2\le m\).
\item \textbf{(Shift calibration.)} \(4(K+2)/m\le 3B/4\).
\end{itemize}
Then
\[
\operatorname{Var}_{Q_P^{(m)}}\!\left(\sum_{k\in S}\widehat P_k\right)
\le
\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{m}
+
C_{\epsilon}6^{2K}
\left\{
dB^2+\frac{d^2K^2B^2}{m}
\right\}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:linear-mark-factorial-covariance}]
Take \(C_\epsilon=64\).  This constant is positive and depends only on \(\epsilon\).
% lean: linear_mark_factorial_covariance

Let
\[
F_k
:=
\widehat P_k
=
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}\frac{g_{Kj}}{B^{j+1}}
\{U_{k,1,j}-U_{k,0,j}\}.
\]
All \(F_k\) have finite second moments under \(Q_P^{(m)}\), since each is a finite linear combination of ordered one-mark factorial statistics and the conditional second-central-moment envelope in \cref{obj:def:model-class} supplies the needed second moments.
% lean: memLp_allBlockLightPolynomialTerm

Expanding the variance of the finite sum gives
\[
\operatorname{Var}_{Q_P^{(m)}}\!\left(\sum_{k\in S}F_k\right)
=
\sum_{k\in S}\sum_{\ell\in S}
\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(F_k,F_\ell).
\]
Consequently,
\[
\operatorname{Var}_{Q_P^{(m)}}\!\left(\sum_{k\in S}F_k\right)
\le
\sum_{k\in S}\sum_{\ell\in S}
\left|\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(F_k,F_\ell)\right|.
\]
% lean: variance_fun_sum'

Fix \(k\in S\), and write \(p_k=P(X=k)\).  The light-cell condition and the shift condition give, for every \(0\le r\le K-2\),
\[
0\le p_k\le B,
\qquad
\frac54p_k\le B,
\qquad
\frac54p_k+\frac{r+2}{m}\le B.
\]
Indeed, \(p_k\le B/4\), while \(r+2\le K\) and \(4(K+2)/m\le3B/4\) control the final summand.  Also \(4(K+2)^2\le m\) gives
\[
\frac{2(K+2)^2}{m}\le \frac12.
\]
For each arm pair and each \(j,r\le K-2\), \cref{obj:lem:same-cell-factorial-cross-moment} therefore yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(U_{k,a,j},U_{k,b,r})\right|
&\le
\frac12\,p_k^{j+2}p_k^{r+2} \\
&\quad+
\exp(1)\left(\frac54p_k\right)^{r+2}
\left(\frac54p_k+\frac{r+2}{m}\right)^{j+2}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Expanding \(F_k\), taking absolute values, and summing over both arm indices decomposes the same-cell contribution into the empty-matching and positive-matching weighted sums.  By \cref{obj:lem:shifted-chebyshev-coefficient-envelope}, the empty-matching part is bounded by
\[
4\cdot\frac12
\left\{
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}
\left|\frac{g_{Kj}}{B^{j+1}}\right|p_k^{j+2}
\right\}
\left\{
\sum_{r=0}^{K-2}
\left|\frac{g_{Kr}}{B^{r+1}}\right|p_k^{r+2}
\right\}
\le
4\cdot\frac12\{B6^K\}^2.
\]
For the positive-matching part, the same coefficient envelope applies once with \((5/4)p_k\) and, for each fixed \(r\), once with \((5/4)p_k+(r+2)/m\); using \(\exp(1)\le3\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
&4\sum_{r=0}^{K-2}
\left|\frac{g_{Kr}}{B^{r+1}}\right|
\exp(1)\left(\frac54p_k\right)^{r+2}
\left\{
\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}
\left|\frac{g_{Kj}}{B^{j+1}}\right|
\left(\frac54p_k+\frac{r+2}{m}\right)^{j+2}
\right\} \\
&\hspace{4em}\le
4\cdot3\{B6^K\}^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus
\[
\left|\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(F_k,F_k)\right|
\le
\left(4\cdot\frac12+4\cdot3\right)\{B6^K\}^2
\le
16\{B6^K\}^2 .
\]
% lean: weighted_centeredCrossMoment_same_cell_sum_le

For distinct \(k,\ell\in S\), \cref{obj:lem:distinct-cell-factorial-cross-moment,obj:lem:shifted-chebyshev-coefficient-envelope} give
\[
\left|\operatorname{Cov}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(F_k,F_\ell)\right|
\le
32\,\frac{K^2}{m}\{B6^K\}^2 .
\]
Here all positive partial matchings between different cells are incompatible, so the only contribution is the empty-matching normalization correction
\[
\frac{2(K+2)^2}{m}p_k^{j+2}p_\ell^{r+2};
\]
summing over both arms contributes the factor \(4\), and \(K\ge2\) gives \((K+2)^2\le4K^2\).
% lean: weighted_centeredCrossMoment_cell_ne_sum_le

Since \(|S|\le d\), summing the diagonal and off-diagonal bounds gives
\[
\operatorname{Var}_{Q_P^{(m)}}\!\left(\sum_{k\in S}F_k\right)
\le
16d\{B6^K\}^2
+
32d^2\frac{K^2}{m}\{B6^K\}^2 .
\]
% lean: linear_mark_factorial_covariance
Using
\[
\{B6^K\}^2=B^2\,6^{2K},
\]
enlarging the two coefficients to \(64\), and adding the nonnegative term \(64/m\), we obtain
\[
\operatorname{Var}_{Q_P^{(m)}}\!\left(\sum_{k\in S}\widehat P_k\right)
\le
\frac{64}{m}
+
64\,6^{2K}
\left\{
dB^2+\frac{d^2K^2B^2}{m}
\right\}.
\]
This is the asserted bound with \(C_\epsilon=64\).
% lean: linear_mark_factorial_covariance
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:linear-mark-factorial-covariance} isolates the continuous-outcome step in the polynomial estimator. The conditional second central moment envelope in \cref{obj:def:model-class} is enough to control the one-mark factorial array after normalization by \(M\), and the final display separates the block sampling term from the two approximation-scale terms that depend on \(d\), \(B\), and \(K\).

The fixed-branch risk bound adds the heavy-cell plug-in component to the light-cell polynomial component. The statement is deterministic in the heavy set \(H\); in the estimator of \cref{obj:def:polynomial-handle}, the sample split allows this result to be applied conditionally on the pilot block.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:polynomial-fixed-branch-risk}[Fixed branch risk bound]
For every overlap parameter \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there is a constant \(C_{\epsilon}>0\) such that the following holds.  Let \(d,m,K\in\mathbb N\), let \(M,\sigma,B,\beta\in\mathbb R\), let \(P\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, and let \(H\subseteq\{1,\ldots,d\}\) be deterministic.  For one block of observations \(O_i=(X_i,A_i,Y_i)\), \(i=1,\ldots,m\), with joint law \(Q_P^{(m)}\), define
\[
\widehat R_H
:=
\sum_{k\in H}
\widehat p_k
\left\{
\widehat\mu_{1k}-\widehat\mu_{0k}
\right\},
\qquad
R_H(P)
:=
\sum_{k\in H}p_k\tau_k,
\]
where \(\widehat p_k\) is the empirical mass of cell \(k\) on the block and \(\widehat\mu_{ak}\) is the arm-cell sample mean on positive arm-cell counts, with the corresponding empty-cell contribution set to zero.  Let \(\widehat P_k\) be the light factorial-polynomial contribution at scale \(B\) and degree \(K\) from \cref{obj:def:polynomial-handle}, and define
\[
\Delta_H
:=
\frac{\widehat R_H-R_H(P)}{M}
+
\left\{
\sum_{k\in H^{\mathrm c}}\widehat P_k
-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k\in H^{\mathrm c}}p_k\tau_k
\right\}.
\]
Let \(\mathcal E_{m,\epsilon,\beta}(H)\) denote the boundary-safe lower-mass missing envelope,
\[
\mathcal E_{m,\epsilon,\beta}(H)
=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{|H|}{\{(m-2)\epsilon/2\}^2\beta},
& \{(m-2)\epsilon/2\}^2\beta>0,\\[1.2ex]
\sum_{k\in H}p_k,
& \{(m-2)\epsilon/2\}^2\beta\le 0.
\end{cases}
\]
Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Degree.)} \(2\le K\).
\item \textbf{(Scales.)} \(B>0\) and \(\beta>0\).
\item \textbf{(Heavy band.)} \(\beta\le p_k\) for every \(k\in H\).
\item \textbf{(Light scale.)} \(p_k\le B/4\) for every \(k\in H^{\mathrm c}\).
\item \textbf{(Block size.)} \(4(K+2)^2\le m\).
\item \textbf{(Shift calibration.)} \(4(K+2)/m\le 3B/4\).
\end{itemize}
Then
\[
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}\!\left[\Delta_H^2\right]
\le
8\left\{
\frac{8\sum_{k\in H}p_k}{(m\vee 1)\epsilon}
+\frac{6}{m\vee 1}
+4\,\mathcal E_{m,\epsilon,\beta}(H)^2
\right\}
+
2\left\{
\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{m}
+C_{\epsilon}6^{2K}
\left(dB^2+\frac{d^2K^2B^2}{m}\right)
+\left(\frac{|H^{\mathrm c}|B}{\epsilon K^2}\right)^2
\right\}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:polynomial-fixed-branch-risk}]
Fix \(\epsilon\in(0,1/2)\), and take \(C_\epsilon>0\) from the covariance bound in \cref{obj:lem:linear-mark-factorial-covariance}; the same constant is used in the fixed light-set calculation below. % lean: fixedLightMarkedPolynomial_error_sq_le

For a sample block \(z\), decompose
\[
\Delta_H(z)=H_m(z)+L_m(z),
\]
where
\[
H_m(z):=\frac{\widehat R_H(z)-R_H(P)}{M}
\]
and
\[
L_m(z):=
\sum_{k\in H^{\mathrm c}}\widehat P_k(z)
-\frac1M\sum_{k\in H^{\mathrm c}}p_k\tau_k .
\]
This is the deterministic fixed-branch normalized error in the statement. % lean: polynomialFixedBranchNormalizedError

The heavy part satisfies
\[
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[H_m^2]
\le
4\left\{
\frac{8\sum_{k\in H}p_k}{(m\vee1)\epsilon}
+\frac{6}{m\vee1}
+4\,\mathcal E_{m,\epsilon,\beta}(H)^2
\right\}.
\]
The fixed marked-ratio bound is applied to the deterministic heavy set \(H\); the category masses in that bound are the cell masses \(p_k\), and the normalization by \(M\) cancels the factor \(M^2\) using \(M\ge1\). % lean: fixedHeavyMarkedRatio_error_sq_le

For the light part, let
\[
\widehat L_S(z):=\sum_{k\in S}\widehat P_k(z),
\qquad
T_S(P):=\frac1M\sum_{k\in S}p_k\tau_k,
\qquad S=H^{\mathrm c}.
\]
The bias-variance identity for the square-integrable statistic \(\widehat L_S\) gives
\[
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[L_m^2]
=
\operatorname{Var}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(\widehat L_S)
+
\left\{
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[\widehat L_S]-T_S(P)
\right\}^2 .
\]
% lean: fixedLightMarkedPolynomial_error_sq_le

The variance term is bounded by \cref{obj:lem:linear-mark-factorial-covariance}:
\[
\operatorname{Var}_{Q_P^{(m)}}(\widehat L_S)
\le
\frac{C_\epsilon}{m}
+
C_\epsilon6^{2K}
\left(dB^2+\frac{d^2K^2B^2}{m}\right),
\]
because \(S=H^{\mathrm c}\) satisfies the light-cell condition \(p_k\le B/4\), together with the stated degree, block-size, and shift-calibration conditions. % lean: linear_mark_factorial_covariance

For the bias term, the block-size condition implies \(K\le m\), so the exact marked-factorial expectation identity applies term by term. With \(s_{1k}=p_k\pi_k\), \(s_{0k}=p_k(1-\pi_k)\), and the coefficients \(g_{Kj}\) from \cref{obj:lem:linear-mark-factorial-covariance}, it gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[\widehat L_S]
&=
\sum_{k\in S}
\Bigg[
 p_k\frac{s_{1k}(\mu_{1k}/M)}{B}
 \sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}\left(\frac{s_{1k}}{B}\right)^j \\
&\hspace{7em}{}-
 p_k\frac{s_{0k}(\mu_{0k}/M)}{B}
 \sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}\left(\frac{s_{0k}}{B}\right)^j
\Bigg].
\end{aligned}
\]
Therefore \(\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[\widehat L_S]-T_S(P)\) is the negative of the sum over \(k\in S\) of
\[
\frac{p_k\tau_k}{M}
-
\Bigg[
 p_k\frac{s_{1k}(\mu_{1k}/M)}{B}
 \sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}\left(\frac{s_{1k}}{B}\right)^j
-
 p_k\frac{s_{0k}(\mu_{0k}/M)}{B}
 \sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}\left(\frac{s_{0k}}{B}\right)^j
\Bigg].
\]
% lean: integral_allBlockMarkedPolynomialSum_eq_population

For \(x\in[0,1]\), the shifted-Chebyshev expansion of the first-kind Chebyshev polynomial gives
\[
T_K(1-2x)
=
1-2K^2x
+2K^2x^2\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}x^j .
\]
Since \(1-2x\in[-1,1]\), the standard Chebyshev bound gives \(|T_K(1-2x)|\le1\), hence \(|1-T_K(1-2x)|\le2\).  Combining this with the preceding identity and using \(K>0\) yields the reciprocal approximation certificate
\[
x\left|1-x\sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}x^j\right|
\le \frac1{K^2}.
\]
% lean: gPolynomial_certificate

For a supported cell, overlap gives \(\epsilon p_k\le s_{ak}\), the propensity range gives \(s_{ak}\le p_k\), the model normalization gives \(|\mu_{ak}/M|\le1/2\), and the light condition gives \(p_k\le B\). Applying the preceding certificate at \(x=s_{ak}/B\) yields, for each arm \(a\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\left|
 p_k\frac{\mu_{ak}}{M}
-
 p_k\frac{s_{ak}(\mu_{ak}/M)}{B}
 \sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}\left(\frac{s_{ak}}{B}\right)^j
\right|
\le
\frac{B}{2\epsilon K^2}.
\]
Combining the two arm bounds gives the cellwise treated-minus-control bias bound
\[
\left|
\frac{p_k\tau_k}{M}
-
\Bigg[
 p_k\frac{s_{1k}(\mu_{1k}/M)}{B}
 \sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}\left(\frac{s_{1k}}{B}\right)^j
-
 p_k\frac{s_{0k}(\mu_{0k}/M)}{B}
 \sum_{j=0}^{K-2}g_{Kj}\left(\frac{s_{0k}}{B}\right)^j
\Bigg]
\right|
\le
\frac{B}{\epsilon K^2}.
\]
When \(p_k=0\), both the target and the displayed population polynomial vanish, so the same bound holds. Summing these cellwise bounds over \(S=H^{\mathrm c}\) gives
\[
\left|
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[\widehat L_S]-T_S(P)
\right|
\le
\frac{|H^{\mathrm c}|B}{\epsilon K^2}.
\]
% lean: polynomialFixedLightPopulationBias_abs_le

Therefore
\[
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[L_m^2]
\le
\frac{C_\epsilon}{m}
+
C_\epsilon6^{2K}
\left(dB^2+\frac{d^2K^2B^2}{m}\right)
+
\left(\frac{|H^{\mathrm c}|B}{\epsilon K^2}\right)^2 .
\]
% lean: fixedLightMarkedPolynomial_error_sq_le

Finally, \((u+v)^2\le2u^2+2v^2\) gives
\[
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[\Delta_H^2]
\le
2\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[H_m^2]
+
2\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(m)}}[L_m^2],
\]
and substituting the two bounds above is exactly the displayed inequality. % lean: polynomialFixedBranchNormalizedError_sq_integral_le
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:polynomial-fixed-branch-risk} decomposes the normalized branch error into a heavy-cell plug-in part, a light-cell polynomial variance part, and an approximation remainder. The boundary-safe envelope records the cost of missing an arm within a heavy cell under the lower-mass band, while the final term \((|H^{\mathrm c}|B/(\epsilon K^2))^2\) is the polynomial approximation contribution.

We next record the full-sample occupancy bound for the collision estimator. The event defining crossed cells in \cref{obj:def:collision-handle} ensures that the treated and control sample means entering the statistic are evaluated only when both arm-cell counts are positive.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:continuous-occupancy-collision-upper-all-d}[Continuous occupancy collision bound]
For every overlap parameter \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there is a constant \(C_{\epsilon}>0\) such that the following holds.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Sample size and alphabet.)} The integers \(n\) and \(d\) satisfy \(n\ge 1\) and \(d\ge 1\).
\item \textbf{(Model.)} The distribution \(P\) belongs to \(\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) from \cref{obj:def:model-class}, with its corresponding bounds on \(M\) and \(\sigma\).
\item \textbf{(Estimator.)} The estimator is the clipped occupancy-weighted treated-control estimator \(T_n^{\mathrm{col}}\) defined in \cref{obj:def:collision-handle} with clipping level \(M\).
\end{itemize}
Then
\[
  E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[\left(T_n^{\mathrm{col}}-\tau(P)\right)^2\right]
  \le
  C_{\epsilon}M^2
  \left(
    \frac{1}{n}
    +\sigma^2
    +\frac{d}{n^2}
  \right).
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:continuous-occupancy-collision-upper-all-d}]
Fix \(\epsilon\in(0,1/2)\).  Let \(b_\epsilon,B_\epsilon>0\) be the constants in \cref{obj:lem:zeng-usable-occupancy-reciprocal}, and set
\[
K_\epsilon:=\frac{16}{\epsilon^2(1-\epsilon)},\qquad
C_\epsilon:=2K_\epsilon B_\epsilon+
\frac{2K_\epsilon B_\epsilon+4}{b_\epsilon}+2 .
\]
These constants are positive.  Now fix \(n,d\ge1\), real parameters \(M,\sigma\), and \(P\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}; let \(Q_P^{(n)}\) be the \(n\)-fold observed-data law. % lean: continuous_occupancy_collision_upper_all_d

Write the unclipped statistic in \cref{obj:def:collision-handle} with its zero fallback as
\[
\widetilde T_n
:=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\frac1{R_n}
\sum_{k:I_k=1}N_k(\overline Y_{1k}-\overline Y_{0k}),& R_n>0,\\[1ex]
0,& R_n=0,
\end{cases}
\]
and write its design-centered version, totalized in the same way, as
\[
C_n
:=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\frac1{R_n}
\sum_{k:I_k=1}N_k(\mu_{1k}-\mu_{0k}),& R_n>0,\\[1ex]
0,& R_n=0.
\end{cases}
\]
On \(\{R_n>0\}\), the difference is the finite-design weighted sum of supported arm-cell residuals
\[
\widetilde T_n-C_n
=
\sum_{k:I_k=1}\sum_{i=1}^n
\frac{N_k}{R_n}
\left\{
\frac{\mathbf 1\{X_i=k,A_i=1\}}{N_{1k}}(Y_i-\mu_{1k})
-
\frac{\mathbf 1\{X_i=k,A_i=0\}}{N_{0k}}(Y_i-\mu_{0k})
\right\},
\]
with value \(0\) on \(\{R_n=0\}\).  The coefficients in this display depend only on the finite design \(((X_i,A_i))_{i=1}^n\).  Expanding the square therefore gives products of two supported residuals multiplied by a product of finite-design coefficients.  When the two sample coordinates are distinct, the product-law integral is zero after conditioning on the finite design of the other coordinates and integrating one coordinate over its arm-cell fibers; the centering used is exactly
\[
\int_{\{X=k,A=a\}}(Y-\mu_{ak})\,dP=0.
\]
When the two factors use the same coordinate but different arm-cell labels, their supports are disjoint, so the product is pointwise zero.  The remaining same-coordinate, same-label terms are bounded by the arm-cell second central moments from \cref{obj:ass:second-central-moment}, and summing the coordinate indicators gives the arm-cell counts.  Hence
\[
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[(\widetilde T_n-C_n)^2\right]
\le
M^2\,
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[
\mathbf 1\{R_n>0\}\frac1{R_n^2}
\sum_{k:I_k=1}N_k^2
\left(\frac1{N_{1k}}+\frac1{N_{0k}}\right)
\right].
\]

It remains to compare the design factor in the last display with reciprocal usable occupancy.  Push the sample to the finite design \(((X_i,A_i))_{i=1}^n\).  In each positive-mass cell, the overlap condition in \cref{obj:ass:overlap} gives both arm probabilities at least \(\epsilon p_k\), equivalently \(\pi_k\in[\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\).  A cell-label configuration that places an observation in a zero-mass cell has zero joint design weight, so it contributes zero before any conditional arm probability is formed.

Fix a positive-mass cell \(k\), a cell-label configuration, and the arm assignments outside \(k\).  Let \(m=N_k\) for this fixed cell-label configuration, and let \(r\) be the usable count contributed by all other cells under the fixed outside arm assignments.  For \(m\le1\), the cell cannot be crossed and its local variance and local reciprocal-occupancy share are both zero.  For \(m\ge2\), summing only over the \(m\) arm assignments inside cell \(k\) gives the local finite-binomial comparison
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{j}\pi_k^j(1-\pi_k)^{m-j}
\frac{m^2}{(r+m)^2}
\mathbf 1\{0<j<m\}
\left(\frac1j+\frac1{m-j}\right) \\
&\qquad\le
\frac{4}{\epsilon^2(1-\epsilon)}
\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{j}\pi_k^j(1-\pi_k)^{m-j}
\frac{m}{(r+m)^2}
\mathbf 1\{0<j<m\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The denominator \((r+m)^2\) is the squared global usable count that applies when cell \(k\) is crossed; it is kept inside the local term throughout the comparison.  The displayed inequality follows from the binomial inverse-arm bound
\[
\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{j}\pi_k^j(1-\pi_k)^{m-j}
\mathbf 1\{0<j<m\}
\left(\frac1j+\frac1{m-j}\right)
\le
\frac{4}{(m+1)\epsilon}
\]
and the interior-mass lower bound
\[
2\epsilon(1-\epsilon)
\le
\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{j}\pi_k^j(1-\pi_k)^{m-j}
\mathbf 1\{0<j<m\}.
\]
Summing the local comparison over cells and outside assignments gives the finite-design inequality with constant \(4/\{\epsilon^2(1-\epsilon)\}\).  We retain the weaker constant \(K_\epsilon=16/\{\epsilon^2(1-\epsilon)\}\), obtaining
\[
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[
\mathbf 1\{R_n>0\}\frac1{R_n^2}
\sum_{k:I_k=1}N_k^2
\left(\frac1{N_{1k}}+\frac1{N_{0k}}\right)
\right]
\le
K_\epsilon\,
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[
\mathbf 1\{R_n>0\}\frac1{R_n}
\right].
\]
Combining the last two displays yields
\[
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[(\widetilde T_n-C_n)^2\right]
\le
K_\epsilon M^2\,
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[
\mathbf 1\{R_n>0\}\frac1{R_n}
\right].
\]
% lean: Causalean.Stat.integral_occupancyWeightedResidual_sq_le_reciprocal

The centered design bias obeys
\[
\mathbb E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[(C_n-\tau(P))^2\right]
\le
M^2\left\{\sigma^2+Q_P^{(n)}(R_n=0)\right\}.
\]
Indeed, because \(Q_P^{(n)}\) is the product observed-data law, every empirically usable cell has positive population mass almost surely: if \(p_k=0\), then no observation falls in cell \(k\) almost surely.  On this full-probability event and on \(\{R_n>0\}\),
\[
C_n-\tau(P)
=
\frac1{R_n}\sum_{k:I_k=1}N_k\delta_k,
\qquad
\delta_k:=\mu_{1k}-\mu_{0k}-\tau(P),
\]
so \(C_n-\tau(P)\) is a convex average of deviations for positive-mass cells.  The homogeneity condition in \cref{obj:ass:approximate-homogeneity} gives \(|C_n-\tau(P)|\le\sigma M\) there.  On \(\{R_n=0\}\), the totalized definition gives \(C_n=0\), and \(|\tau(P)|\le M\) by \cref{obj:lem:scale-sanity}. % lean: integral_collisionDesignCenter_bias_sq_le

Put
\[
x:=\frac{n\vee d}{n^2}.
\]
